Emotional Intelligence: The Secret to Getting Ahead at Work


Description: Emotional Intelligence: The Secret to Getting Ahead at Work
Lyra Session OPEN to ALL GOOGLERS


Skill building with GAIN x LYRA for World Indigenous Peoples' Week (go/wipw026)


Technical brilliance gets you in the door, but emotional intelligence unlocks true workplace influence. In this interactive session, master the art of recognising, regulating, and expressing emotions productively. You'll gain practical tools to navigate complex dynamics, strengthen team collaboration, and elevate your career while bringing your most authentic, resilient self to work daily.


GAIN is proud to offer these workshops as an opportunity for Googlers to invest in their personal and professional growth through essential skill-building. As an ERG rooted in community care, we know that cultivating emotional agility and sustainable work habits is vital for protecting our well-being and empowering our members to thrive holistically.


Attachments: Emotional-Intelligence-Workshop-Handout.pdf


Attendees: Pedro Correia (pcorreia@google.com)


Meeting notes:


Notes:
* Emotional intelligence (EQ) is defined as the ability to strategically manage emotions and interpersonal relationships—even during stressful circumstances—to become a more effective leader and team member.
* There are four primary skills associated with EQ:
   * Self-awareness: Knowledge of what you are feeling and the effect you have on others.
   * Self-management: How you act in response to or in anticipation of your emotions.
   * Situational awareness: Using empathy and observation to adapt your behaviors.
   * Relationship management: Strategies to create productive and fulfilling connections.
* The benefits of high emotional intelligence include greater job satisfaction, stronger relationships, increased well-being, higher income, and better team performance.
* Emotional intelligence is not correlated with gender and can be increased by anyone through ongoing practice and feedback.
* Key strategies for self-awareness involve recognizing your typical emotional responses and anticipating situations that provoke them.
* To practice self-management, use a three-step process: become aware of your emotions, name them without shame, and pause to consider how you want to demonstrate your qualities.
* Situational awareness can be strengthened by observing how others communicate, seeing situations from their perspective, and avoiding assumptions.
* Relationship management involves identifying willing partners for collaboration and taking responsibility for one's own role in workplace conflicts.

JB Shopping Agent | Google -Adhoc Connect
Description: Hi team,


Since we are closing in on the launch deadline on Aug 18th . Looking at the pending Items in the issue tracker . I have increased the cadence of this meeting daily only for the current week , SO we can have a quick analysis over all the issues and a recurrent feedback loop before the launch


Monday -- Store launch changes from JB Hifi


Attachments: JB Shopping Agent Document


Attendees: Nik Gravina (nik.gravina@jbhifi.com.au), George Smith (george.smith@jbhifi.com.au), Joeri Ovart (joeriovart@google.com), Saurabh Soni (sonsau@google.com), Aman Sabir (amansabir@google.com), Zack (zack.petrov@jbhifi.com.au), Shashank Sambana (spranays@google.com), Himanshu Malik (himanshu.malik@jbhifi.com.au), Pedro Correia (pcorreia@google.com)
Meeting notes: Meeting Notes Document


Notes:


Summary
The meeting finalized Phase 1 project scopes and prioritized local inventory data ingestion via AICS infrastructure.


Defining Phase 1 Scope


Critical User Journeys for Phase 1 are confirmed as manageable using existing data sources. Backend requirements for search, filtering, and cart integration were aligned for immediate implementation.


Prioritizing Inventory Data Ingestion


The team decided to prioritize local inventory data ingestion in Phase 1 to improve performance and reduce latency. This shift replaces the reliance on real-time API calls for stock checks.


Managing Future Complex Integrations


Complex service attachments and non-standard logic are deferred to later phases to prioritize speed. Brand tone and user acceptance testing will continue to evolve through iterative refinement.


Decisions


Aligned
* Grounding restricted to help pages: The agent's information grounding is restricted to the website's "/pages/help-and-support" directory.
* Scope strategy set for Phase 1.5: The project scope is defined with Phase 1 focusing on out-of-the-box functionality, while complex or custom requirements are relegated to a "fast fallout" Phase 1.5 stream.
* Mobile application support excluded: Mobile application support is excluded from the current project scope, with the implementation limited to the web interface.


Next steps
* [ ] [Shashank] Share Cart API Expectations: Draft and distribute the requirements for the Cart API integration. Include expectations for callbacks and list cart functionality.
* [ ] [Nik Gravina, Paige Ngo, Zack] Estimate Stock Pipeline Effort: Review provided documentation to determine the level of effort required for building data pipelines for local inventory.
* [ ] [Bhawna Kumari] Share Data Ingestion Guides: Provide documentation and guidance on mapping specific product data to the standard schema for Google AICS.
* [ ] [Ritesh Singla] Schedule Follow Up Session: Organize a meeting to discuss the Cart, Installation, and Delivery APIs once documentation and requirements are shared.
* [ ] [Ritesh Singla] Share AICS details: Provide all existing Artificial Intelligence Commerce Solution details and recommendations. Include data for live in store availability.
* [ ] [Joeri Ovart] Consult legal team: Contact the legal department to resolve pending legal inquiries.
* [ ] [Joeri Ovart, Shashank, Ritesh Singla] Follow up UX UI: Engage the User Experience and User Interface team regarding product tile configuration and identified bugs.
* [ ] [Bhawna Kumari] Verify deployment: Confirm if tone of voice changes are correctly reflected in the current deployment. Provide feedback if further revisions are required.
* [x] [George Smith] Submit UI changes: Communicate any requested modifications to the look and feel of the user interface to Shashank.
* [x] [Andrew McCrohan] Enable note sharing: Demonstrate the settings for automatically sharing meeting notes to Joeri Ovart.


Details
* Phase One Scope and Initial Planning: Ritesh Singla initiated the review of the Critical User Journeys divided into phases, confirming that Phase One items are expected to function out of the box using existing data sources within AICS.
* Standard Search and Filter Functionality: Standard search and filter requests should function immediately provided the data in AICS is correctly ingested.
* Custom Attribute Requirements: Bhawna Kumari requested a list of all custom attributes currently ingested in AICS to ensure the agent supports necessary filtering.
* Store Hours Grounding: Store hours grounding was discussed to ensure accurate, low-level detail retrieval from website store information pages.
* Shopping Cart Integration: Add to Cart functionality is handled via client callbacks rather than backend-to-backend API calls.
* Checkout and Cart Navigation: George Smith requested the ability to toggle off the "Go to Checkout" functionality in certain scenarios.
* Product Recommendations and Similar Items: Frequently bought together recommendations are already configured in serving configs.
* Content Grounding and Denial Lists: Grounding is restricted to the help and support section of the website.
* Live In-Store Availability: Ingesting local inventory data into the AICS standard schema allows the agent to effectively filter for stock availability.
* Feasibility of Stock Data Ingestion: Andrew McCrohan suggested that data pipelines take approximately 3 to 4 weeks and significantly reduce latency.
* Service Attachments and Delivery Methods: Service attachments involve complex logic, and teams agreed to review documentation and API contracts before finalizing the approach.
* Tone of Voice and UAT Agent: Joeri Ovart suggested building a UAT agent to experiment with the brand's tone of voice.
* Product Tile Flexibility: Nik Gravina noted that requirements for product tiles were already submitted, awaiting UX feedback on the pricing box.
* Mobile Strategy: The focus remains exclusively on web-based implementation as JB Hi-Fi does not currently have a mobile app.


Pedro <> Nick: Weekly
Description:


Attachments:Pedro <> Nick: Weekly Document


Attendees: Nick Jenkins (nickjenkins@google.com), Pedro Correia (pcorreia@google.com)
Meeting notes:


Notes:


Summary
Discussion reviewed Ready Tech account metrics alongside strategic sales methodology shifts and complex financial modeling challenges.


Ready Tech Account Strategy


The client shows increased consumption but lacks agent governance readiness, prompting a focus on provisioned throughput to avoid technical issues. Sales strategies now prioritize pre-configured environments to demonstrate functionality.


Deal Financials and Losses


The 3.7 model significantly reduced Matt Track deal value, complicating revenue projections and quota attainment. Recent losses including the Megaport and Prei deals highlighted critical partner and internal champion dependencies.


Sales Process Evolution


Current training methods are viewed as reactionary and insufficient, driving a shift toward hands-on environment provisioning for clients. Future engagement requires cautious workload sizing based on baseline consumption analysis.


Decisions


Needs Further Discussion
* GSU commitment strategy evaluation The strategy for determining initial GSU commitments is under review, with a proposal to adopt a cautious approach by starting with a small baseline commitment (e.g., 10%) before scaling, though further commercial validation is required.


Aligned
* Shared demo instance strategy adoption The team decided to adopt a centralized demo environment strategy by deploying common connectors—such as Monday.com, Slack, and Jira—to a shared instance, rather than requiring clients to build these connections themselves.


Next steps
* [ ] Draft Follow-up: Draft the follow-up email to the Ready Tech team to confirm availability for the provisioned throughput.
* [ ] Review Draft: Review the follow-up email draft prepared for the Ready Tech team.
* [ ] Get Meeting Materials: Request the meeting recording or notes from the Ready Tech session from Chris.
* [ ] Call Athena: Contact Athena to discuss the current project status and determine next steps.
* [ ] Analyze GSU Requirements: Consult with Ashin to analyze the average input and output usage to determine GSU requirements for the Metra project.


Details
* Training and Organizational Strategy: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) expressed frustration regarding the current training schedule, noting that the business approach is reactionary rather than preemptive and lacks adequate structure. They highlighted that the timeline for content delivery was compressed, creating significant pressure and opportunity costs for sales staff. Someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) noted that despite providing feedback in Singapore about extending the training over a longer period to manage updates, their concerns were dismissed. The consensus is that the training acts as a "tick box" exercise, serving to push Field Sales Representatives toward becoming AI specialists or risk job obsolescence as the business shifts focus toward AI revenue.
* Wellness and Work-Life Balance: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) acknowledged the need for better sleep and work-life balance, noting that previous habits of functioning on minimal sleep led to unsustainable baseline stress levels.
* Ready Tech - Consumption Metrics: Regarding the Ready Tech account, someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) reported that spend is increasing, with consumption hitting 1,800 units by August 12th. They noted that while 3.5 Flash is currently being utilized as the model, it is not the best available option, and they acknowledged that 3.7 is a significant step forward.
* Future Model Outlook: The discussion turned to future model releases, with someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) noting the ominous nature of comments regarding Gemini 4, while concluding that the 3.5 Pro model may not be released.
* Ready Tech - Governance and Throughput: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) observed that the client, specifically the product and technical leads (Chris and Ashin), is not yet ready for agent governance. The discussion emphasized that while the client is interested in controlling agents, cost and the availability of models within Australia take precedence. Regarding the use of 3.5 via provisioned throughput, someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) suggested prioritizing this method to avoid technical flakiness associated with local model constraints, despite it not being strictly required.
* Ready Tech - Modeling Savings: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) described a successful walkthrough of a consumption model with the client, utilizing a waterfall chart to illustrate the benefits of batch jobs, provisioned throughput, and a 40% discount. The client is currently awaiting a date for 3.5 provisioned throughput, and someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) identified the need to return to the client to size the requirement accurately.
* Ready Tech - Blockers and Feedback: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) discussed persistent blockers, specifically data residency requirements and the inability to retire commits as one can on AWS. They noted that the client is keenly interested in change management features, as they anticipate growth in their orchestration usage, and confirmed that current Anthropic spend is mostly internal.
* Ready Tech - Technical Stakeholder Engagement: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) reflected on the involvement of the client's technical team, noting that the Head of Platform (Dale) did not contribute to the conversation, while Chris and Ashin were the primary stakeholders asking relevant questions.
* Ready Tech - Follow-up Strategy: To maintain momentum, someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) committed to drafting a follow-up email to the Ready Tech team, which will include the Gemini enterprise agent platform information. They also requested to obtain the meeting recording or notes from the client.
* Matt Track - Financials: For the Matt Track deal, someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) noted that the 3.7 model cannibalizes the majority of the deal value. The original budget of 2 to 3 million dollars was priced with 3.5 at roughly 1.1 million, which dropped to 800k with 3.7 and further to 500k after applying a 50% discount. The deal involves a credit draw-down mechanism, where consumption leads to credits in the following month, though this does not count toward 100% quota attainment.
* Matt Track - Next Steps: The next phase for the Matt Track deal involves sizing the engagement, specifically balancing Google Service Units against pay-as-you-go options. Someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) plans to finalize this balance and verify the calculator inputs before proceeding.
* Deal Losses: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) reported the loss of the Megaport deal, attributing 70% of the failure to the partner, Geeks on Tap. Additionally, the Prei deal is considered dead, as the Chief AI Officer, who served as their internal champion, is leaving the company.
* Sales Methodology - Shift in Approach: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) discussed moving away from "pilot deals" where clients are expected to set up instances independently, as this approach is not yielding results. They proposed a new strategy where sales staff pre-configure instances, spin up projects, and set up agents for the client using a Google Enterprise use case simulator, which allows clients to log in to a fully prepared environment.
* Sales Methodology - Overcoming Data Connector Limitations: A challenge identified with the pre-configured environment approach is the difficulty of setting up specific third-party data connectors, such as Monday.com. Someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) suggested utilizing dummy accounts and dummy data to demonstrate functionality rather than requiring live integrations during initial demos.
* Environment Provisioning: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) explored the possibility of bootstrapping Gemini Enterprise instances with pre-configured groups, calendars, and Gmail connectors to streamline the demonstration process. They suggested that this task should be a collaborative effort, potentially involving Scotty to provide expertise on demo structure.
* Emerging Tools and Deployment: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) highlighted new tools such as the "Spark" computer use feature and an agent builder, noting that these tools are evolving to assist in browser-based tasks. They also referenced a "one-click deploy" virtual machine tool built by James Parkin, which allows for no-code environment creation and support bot deployment.
* Athena Follow-up: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) acknowledged the need to follow up with Athena to address the loss of momentum and ensure the client remains engaged.
* Financial Modeling Breakdown: someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) explained the structure of the consumption model used with clients, which includes scenarios for batch, synchronous, and chat workloads, accounting for factors like Committed Use Discounts and pay-as-you-go flexibility.
* Sizing New Workloads: To size new workloads, someone in Hamilton (au-syd-pir) recommended a cautious approach, suggesting they sit with the client to analyze input/output averages and query frequency. They proposed starting with a small commitment—roughly 10% of estimated consumption—to establish a baseline, which can then be adjusted as actual usage data becomes available.

Meeting records Transcript


Summary
The meeting finalized Phase 1 project scopes and prioritized local inventory data ingestion via AICS infrastructure.


Defining Phase 1 Scope


Critical User Journeys for Phase 1 are confirmed as manageable using existing data sources. Backend requirements for search, filtering, and cart integration were aligned for immediate implementation.


Prioritizing Inventory Data Ingestion


The team decided to prioritize local inventory data ingestion in Phase 1 to improve performance and reduce latency. This shift replaces the reliance on real-time API calls for stock checks.


Managing Future Complex Integrations


Complex service attachments and non-standard logic are deferred to later phases to prioritize speed. Brand tone and user acceptance testing will continue to evolve through iterative refinement.


Decisions


Aligned
* Grounding restricted to help pages The agent's information grounding is restricted to the website's "/pages/help-and-support" directory.
* Scope strategy set for Phase 1.5 The project scope is defined with Phase 1 focusing on out-of-the-box functionality, while complex or custom requirements are relegated to a "fast fallout" Phase 1.5 stream.
* Mobile application support excluded Mobile application support is excluded from the current project scope, with the implementation limited to the web interface.

Details
* Phase One Scope and Initial Planning: Ritesh Singla initiated the review of the Critical User Journeys (CUJs) divided into phases, confirming that the "Phase One" items are expected to function out of the box using existing data sources within AICS. The team, including Nik Gravina and Joeri Ovart, reviewed these items and reached a consensus that the primary scope for Phase One is straightforward and manageable ([REDACTED URL]) ([REDACTED URL]).
* Standard Search and Filter Functionality: Ritesh Singla confirmed that standard search and filter requests, such as finding specific products like noise-canceling headphones or laptops with 16GB RAM, should function immediately provided the data in AICS is correctly ingested ([REDACTED URL]). For more complex queries involving product variants like 65-inch TVs, the team noted these should work as expected if the backend contains appropriate tagging, though additional work may be necessary if that data is missing ([REDACTED URL]).
* Custom Attribute Requirements: Bhawna Kumari requested a list of all custom attributes currently ingested in AICS to ensure the agent supports necessary filtering, particularly for non-standard retail attributes like sale indicators ([REDACTED URL]). Gehan Ranawana and Nik Gravina noted that while mapping to standard attributes exists, they will provide examples of specific custom product data from the Vertex platform in the chat to clarify these needs ([REDACTED URL]).
* Store Hours Grounding: The team discussed the implementation of store hour queries, with Ritesh Singla noting that the JB Hi-Fi website serves as the current data source. Gehan Ranawana mentioned that store hours are also ingested into Google Maps, and Bhawna Kumari suggested grounding the agent on a specific store information page to ensure accurate, low-level detail retrieval ([REDACTED URL]).
* Shopping Cart Integration: Regarding "Add to Cart" functionality, Bhawna Kumari explained that this is handled via client callbacks rather than backend-to-backend API calls ([REDACTED URL]). Zack noted that the current cart state can be accessed via the default Shopify cart.js endpoint, and Ritesh Singla tasked Shashank with sharing the technical expectations post-meeting to ensure these requirements are met for the CUJs ([REDACTED URL]).
* Checkout and Cart Navigation: George Smith requested the ability to toggle off the "Go to Checkout" functionality in certain scenarios to avoid bypassing the cart when customer information needs to be presented ([REDACTED URL]). Ritesh Singla agreed that this configuration would be necessary, similar to other flexible settings for landing pages.
* Product Recommendations and Similar Items: Ritesh Singla discussed the "My TV" recommendation scenario, with Bhawna Kumari explaining that this could be handled by configuring serving configs in AICS to display recommended products or add-ons ([REDACTED URL]). Nik Gravina confirmed that "frequently bought together" recommendations are already configured, while other specific requests like wall mounts are deferred to Phase 1.5 ([REDACTED URL]).
* Content Grounding and Denial Lists: For queries involving technical comparisons like OLED versus QLED, Ritesh Singla emphasized the need for specific, reliable website sources. Bhawna Kumari suggested implementing a "denial list" for stale pages or customer-generated content, and Nik Gravina confirmed that grounding would be restricted to the help and support section of the website ([REDACTED URL]).
* Live In-Store Availability: The group debated moving "Live In-Store Availability" (e.g., checking stock at Chadston) from Phase 1.5 to Phase 1. Bhawna Kumari noted that if local inventory data—including store location, price, and custom attributes—is ingested into the AICS standard schema, the agent can effectively filter for unavailable stock ([REDACTED URL]).
* Feasibility of Stock Data Ingestion: Nik Gravina and Joeri Ovart questioned the effort required to implement this ingestion to prioritize it for Phase 1 ([REDACTED URL]). Andrew McCrohan suggested that with proper data pipelines, the effort is manageable—approximately 3 to 4 weeks—and significantly reduces latency compared to real-time API calls, leading the team to agree to investigate the feasibility of moving this to Phase 1 ([REDACTED URL]).
* Service Attachments and Delivery Methods: Discussions regarding service attachments, such as installations and delivery, highlighted that these processes involve complex logic rather than simple one-to-one product relationships ([REDACTED URL]) ([REDACTED URL]). Ritesh Singla and Bhawna Kumari advocated for AICS data ingestion as the preferred approach for performance, though Andrew McCrohan proposed a "sub-agent" as an alternative if the logic proves too complex for a standard ingestion ([REDACTED URL]) ([REDACTED URL]).
* Future-Proofing Services via Sub-Agents: Joeri Ovart supported the sub-agent approach for installations, noting it offers flexibility for future updates ([REDACTED URL]). Bhawna Kumari clarified that AICS's "fulfillment type" field might still support these requirements natively, but the team agreed to review documentation and API contracts before finalizing the approach ([REDACTED URL]).
* Strategy for Complex Integrations: Ritesh Singla and Paige Ngo acknowledged the trade-off between speed and system complexity, agreeing to reserve complex API integrations for Phase 1.5 if immediate AICS ingestion is not feasible ([REDACTED URL]). The team confirmed they would iterate on image-based queries, such as wall mount compatibility, during Phase 1.5 following initial success ([REDACTED URL]).

Live and Labs program planning session addressed regional event logistics and staffing requirements for cloud AI.


Program goals and agenda


The annual program targets technical decision makers to boost cloud AI demand across 3 regional cities. Planned activations include keynotes, specialized demos, and networking opportunities to engage attendees.


Staffing and venue logistics


Staff must manage guest transitions between venues and maintain designated stations to ensure smooth operations. High attendance expectations necessitate strict adherence to arrival times and specific dress codes.


Lab support and structure


Sequential lab sessions require staff support for roaming and technical guidance. Staff are requested to sign up for remaining open slots to meet capacity requirements.


Decisions
        Event staff dress code: All staff members are required to wear Google Cloud black tees or smart business casual attire for the event.
        Transition staffing protocol: Staff must remain in their assigned positions during the 20-minute transition between venues to ensure efficient guest movement.
        Sequential lab session structure: Lab sessions will be conducted sequentially, with the beginner lab followed by the secondary lab, to effectively manage facility capacity constraints.Next steps

Extracted slide notes and details from the training database:
   * The frontal lobe functions as the brain's command center, handling decision-making, planning, and problem-solving.
   * Brain plasticity (neuroplasticity) drives memory creation and learning, operating on the principle that neurons firing together wire together.
   * Machine learning enables systems to automatically learn and improve from experience without explicit programming.
   * Deep learning utilizes multi-layered artificial neural networks inspired by human neural structures.
   * Self-leadership serves as the foundation for leading others, starting with self-awareness across six core mindsets.
   * Deepfake technology leverages Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) to combine images and videos.
   * Traditional TV upfront buying faces challenges from declining audiences and limited digital agility compared to streaming alternatives.
   * Motivation spans intrinsic factors (such as instinct, drive, and arousal) and extrinsic factors (such as incentives and expectancy).
   * The Ringelmann Effect demonstrates that larger groups can reduce individual effort and accountability.
   * Successful negotiation relies on preparation, active listening (following the 70/30 rule), assertiveness, and understanding counterpart pressures.
   * Positive psychology notes that 90% of long-term happiness is shaped by how the brain processes the world, and maintaining a high positive-to-negative feedback ratio drives high performance.
   * Augmented reality (AR) superimposes digital content onto live camera feeds using computer vision and 3D rendering.
   * Binary code uses 1s and 0s (bits) as the foundation for modern computing instructions.
   * Trust is built in small moments through the BRAVING framework (Boundaries, Reliability, Accountability, Vault, Integrity, Non-judgment, Generous).
   * Effective presentations maximize impact at the beginning and end, rely on concise big ideas, and require rigorous rehearsal.
   * Human decision-making relies on heuristics and intuition under uncertainty, whereas AI excels at consistent statistical processing.
   * Creative briefs act as short-form communication tools to establish clear campaign missions and inspire teams.
   * The Dunning-Kruger Effect shows that low performers tend to over-estimate their competence, while top performers often under-rate themselves.
   * Social media usage continues to expand, reshaping news consumption, commerce, and digital communication.
   * Google Maps has evolved over 15 years into an AI-driven, immersive navigation and contextual discovery platform.

Meeting records Transcript


Summary
The team aligned on launch strategies, finalized pricing display logic, and prioritized core system stability for release.


Warranty and Branding Updates


Warranty responses default to a generic phone contact for launch consistency. Branding and tone updates were confirmed alongside the removal of unused image upload features.


Pricing Logic and Infrastructure


Pricing display rules were finalized to restrict strike-through text exclusively to products with active sale tags. System infrastructure and log access updates are progressing for testing environments.


Launch Strategy and Limitations


The rollout is scheduled for Wednesday with a 1 to 5 percent traffic ramp-up strategy. Complex tracking events remain pending while Product Detail Page prompts are migrating to production.


Decisions


Aligned
   * Consumer law link hyperlinking The consumer law reference within the legal copy is formatted as a hyperlink.

Databases Masterclass - Scaling Down the Bill: Bigtable Cost Optimization Strategies at Spotify
Description: Managing cost efficiency in Bigtable often presents a greater challenge than scaling infrastructure as data footprints expand. This session explores the essential levers of Bigtable cost optimization, covering key architectural strategies like data lifecycle management, storage-to-compute balancing, and autoscaling configurations. Additionally, we will examine how recent feature additions—including tiered storage and caching—can effectively reduce total cost of ownership (TCO) and eliminate unnecessary spend. To illustrate these practices in a real-world setting, we will review a Spotify production case study, examining how they audited their clusters and leveraged these features to deliver substantial savings. Whether overseeing a few nodes or a large global deployment, attendees will gain actionable technical insights and a practical guide to maximize Bigtable ROI.

Atlassian FDE Kick-off 🚀🚀
Description: Hi team,


As per email and chat, I've scheduled this kick off to run through the Atlassian account, key workloads, and upcoming next steps for our work with Atlassian.


Kind Regards,
Emily


Attachments: Meeting Document


Attendees: Emily Qiao (emilyqiao@google.com), Lewis Smith (lewiswsmith@google.com), Tanya Dixit (dixittanya@google.com), Mohamed Kabiruddin (mokabir@google.com), Pouya Ghiasnezhad Omran (pgomran@google.com)
Meeting notes: Document Notes by Gemini


Notes:
Atlassian Partnership and Architecture
            * Atlassian operates as a parent company for a teamwork collection including Jira, Confluence, Trello, Loom, and The Browser Company.
            * The 7-year, $700 million Google partnership originated with the Bluebird project, focused on migrating Atlassian services from AWS to GCP.
            * Atlassian utilizes a centralized GCP project architecture, which streamlines engagement by consolidating API and infrastructure management through a single platform team.
AI Strategic Objectives
            * The primary goal is converting remaining third-party (Claude) traffic to Gemini to optimize costs and performance.
            * Robo Chat accounts for 80-90% of token traffic and remains the immediate priority for migration and optimization efforts.
            * Support for product integrations in Jira and Loom is critical to increasing market presence and expanding Gemini adoption.
            * Long-term infrastructure strategy considers potential shifts toward open-source models on hosted GPUs, though Cloud AI remains the current revenue priority.
Teamwork Collection Workloads
            * The Browser Company (DIA) currently uses Claude Opus; Gemini migration requires significant work on harness and API compatibility.
            * Jira AI team is prioritizing high-visibility features, including natural language to JQL conversions and AI-managed agents.
            * Agentic Search requires LLM integration for ranking and query classification, with engineering interest in model distillation and fine-tuning.
            * Robo Chat optimization centers on quality, latency, reliability, and cost management across 16 distinct tier and mode configurations.
            * Loom priorities include advancing multimodal transcription, video understanding, scene detection, and agent-created loom features.
            * Confluence is addressing quality issues in Gemini-generated diagramming and exploring fine-tuning models for Confluence Query Language (CQL).
Engagement and Resource Planning
            * The FTE team is positioned to bridge the gap between product and sales by building custom product integrations.
            * Engagement relies on discovery calls to define specific scopes and secure equivalent engineering counterparts on the client side.
            * Current resources are constrained; leadership is actively evaluating team expansion or workload prioritization to meet demand.
Suggested next steps
            * Ed Muthiah: Create Support Document: Compile a comprehensive document outlining potential support areas for the Robo Chat team. Include details on infrastructure and performance optimization opportunities.
            * The group: Define Repo Ownership: Establish clear ownership for repositories and solutions concerning AI product integrations with Atlassian. Identify which internal teams or partners are responsible for maintaining the code.
            * The group: Organize Kickoff Meetings: Schedule individual kickoff sessions for every identified Atlassian workload and opportunity. Define the scope and objectives for each conversation prior to team meetings.
            * The group: Scope Browser Engagement: Determine the starting points and engagement scope for the Browser Company project with Lewis, Tanya, Pouya, and the engineers. Formalize the technical requirements and collaboration structure.
            * Someone in Terrain (au-syd-pir): Update Chat Access: Add team members to relevant Atlassian internal G chat channels for project workloads.
            * Mo, Ed Muthiah: Manage Laptop Access: Facilitate legal processes and system access requirements for Atlassian hardware.
            * Someone in Terrain (au-syd-pir): Raise Expert Requests: Submit official expert requests for projects that are currently pending documentation.
            * The group: Conduct Deep Dives: Facilitate detailed sessions to review project workloads and requirements throughout the coming days.
            * The group: Standardize Engagement Planning: Implement a standardized project plan template across all engagements to establish scope and resource estimates.
            * Ed Muthiah, Tanya Dixit: Provide Project Clarity: Offer detailed orientation and project context to Lewis during scheduled kickoff meetings.

               * Default GCP resources discussed are GCE CPUs, GPUs and Storage (i.e., PD, Local SSD).
               * Items to be discussed should be entered at least 2 hours ahead of time to allow non-required parties to opt out of attending and reduce the overall time of the meeting.

________________


Summary


Workflow discussion centered on leveraging Comfy UI over Creative Studio for complex campaigns, referencing the YouTube ABCD framework, and detailing post-production methods.
               * Workflow Uses Comfy UI: Creative Studio is a good content starting point, but Comfy UI is preferred for managing multiple workflows like display campaigns and YouTube videos. The initial creation process involves an agent that defines the essential parameters for content generation.
               * Structured Prompts and Post-Production: Post-production uses Vids to stitch together short video segments after generating longer footage to ensure selection of the perfect clip. A separate agent creates structured JSON prompts that allow for better versioning and modification of output details, such as correcting an erroneous steering wheel placement.
               * Video Consistency Deficiencies Noted: Scott Zanevra noted two major deficiencies: 4K quality is insufficient for large screens, and character consistency is not 100% reliable within the current video generation process. They advised using a specific project for video generation due to fewer restrictions and better billing management.
Next steps
               * Pedro Correia will put the workflow demonstration, agent, and prompt details (V3 prompt expert) into the slide pack for Scott Zanevra to replicate and build on top of.
               * Pedro Correia will share the agent and/or the prompt with Scott Zanevra for him to create it in Gemini.
               * Scott Zanevra will attempt to build out the ad from end to end according to JK's brief, give playback, and mention the deficiencies.
               * Pedro Correia will share a copy of the slides (showing IG sort of example, ad script generator, characters created, and video) with Scott Zanevra.
               * Scott Zanevra will have a crack at doing what Pedro Correia just did.
Details
               * Initial Workflow Discussion and Tool Comparison: The conversation established that Creative Studio is a good starting point for content creation, but Comfy UI is better suited for handling multiple different workflows, such as display campaigns or YouTube videos. Pedro Correia confirmed that the initial process includes a step that is part of their workflow, likely involving an agent to define what is important for the creation.
               * Sharing and Replication of the Scene Workflow: Scott Zanevra requested that Pedro Correia include the scene following the YouTube ABCD framework into a slide pack so they could try to simulate and replicate the process. Pedro Correia agreed to share the information after the call, noting that the agent defines the different characters and how to create them, acting as the first step in the process.
               * Video Generation Tools and Control: Pedro Correia explained that after the script is available, one can use Vert.XCI Studio to generate a new video by referencing the video and uploading desired images. They mentioned that they prefer not to use native VO integration because they require greater control over parameters like aspect ratio, duration in seconds, and resolution, and they prefer to drop content in a GCS bucket.
               * Comfy UI and Local Environment Setup: Scott Zanevra confirmed that Comfy UI functions as a wrapper around the service, and Pedro Correia mentioned that Comfy UI can be installed and run locally, which is their current setup. Because there are no native nodes connecting Comfy UI to Vertex, a GitHub repository wrapper is leveraged, but Pedro Correia created a new node in their local environment to access the updated models like nano banana.
               * Video Stitching and Post-Production: Pedro Correia described their post-production process using Vids, where they take the short video segments (e.g., 4 seconds or 2 seconds of good quality footage) and stitch them together, noting that the script helps define the duration of each scene. They explained that they generate videos longer than needed (e.g., 8 seconds) and then cut them down in Vids to select the perfect segment.
               * Structured Prompt Generation using Agents: Pedro Correia detailed the use of a separate agent that creates prompts in a JSON structure, which is more effective than using simple prompts. This structured output, when dropped into the system, expands the information and allows for better versioning and modification of the prompt, such as changing details if the initial output (e.g., a steering wheel being on the wrong side) is incorrect.
               * Audio Generation and Project Sharing: Regarding audio generation, Pedro Correia noted that they typically generate audio to demonstrate the capability, but they can choose not to generate it by applying a filter. They agreed to share the structured prompt agent with Scott Zanevra, but clarified that they would need to share the prompt rather than the agent itself due to sharing complexities on their internal platform.
               * Video Quality and Consistency Deficiencies: Scott Zanevra noted key deficiencies in the current video generation process, including the quality not being sufficient for large screens (4K not being enough) and character consistency not being 100%. Furthermore, it was noted that voice-overs work well, but the current process cannot apply instant voice models to the generated voice for scenes where characters need to speak.
               * Next Steps and Project Recommendations: Scott Zanevra confirmed their next steps would involve building the ad based on the brief and mentioning the identified deficiencies to stakeholders. Pedro Correia advised using a specific project for video generation rather than their general project list, as it has fewer restrictions and helps manage billing costs. They emphasized the importance of using "reference to video" (not "image to video") where characters are generated in nano banana and then added to the reference.

               * Client status review centered on Temple and Webster’s underwhelming Gemini Enterprise experience, necessitating a product roadmap discussion and agent adoption strategy, with an immediate deferral of the shopping agent.

The complex internal approval process and risk assessment led to the decision to avoid prioritizing the shopping agent with Temple and Webster at this time.
               * Agent Adoption Strategy: A joint session is planned to address the roadmap and organizational adoption, focusing on establishing “day zero value” by teaching a "mini champions group" how to create and connect low-code agents. Details
               * Client Accounts and Immediate Focus: The key client accounts previously supported were Tick Techch, Templan Webster, and Iconic, with some work also related to Finder. Temple and Webster, the most urgent client, signed up for 20 Gemini Enterprise licenses last year and have been underwhelmed with the product's day-one value. Previous partner efforts on onboarding (Aviato) and an agent proof of concept for returns management did not progress well, as the agent never reached production.
               * Temple and Webster Strategy and Agent Challenges: The primary goal is a roadmap session to generate excitement about the future direction of Gemini Enterprise to address client dissatisfaction and increase license acquisition. A secondary priority is assisting with the 80k agent building efforts, particularly unsticking the development of a homegrown data analyst agent that queries BigQuery, which the client is porting to the ADK platform. The client’s CTO desired a standardized agent platform, but the team is experiencing teething issues integrating components with Gemini Enterprise and troubleshooting features like sessions.
               * Gemini Enterprise Connectors and Agent Development: Temple and Webster are currently using some Atlassian and G Suite/Workspace connectors, but no custom connectors are in use yet. They have been primarily focused on agent building but have also been underwhelmed by the capabilities of the no-code agent builder. The client's CEO and founder, Adam, wanted to control the proliferation of agents being developed to promote reuse.
               * Shopping Agent Demonstration and Access Challenges: A demonstration of the shopping agent using the client's merchant center data was conducted with the co-founder and CTO, who expressed keen interest. Access to the shopping agent is complicated by an internal process requiring a committed customer, a clear path to production rollout, and an aligned delivery team. The co-founder is reportedly conservative and skeptical about the out-of-the-box value, especially since their business involves large ticket items, making augmented reality potentially a better investment than agentic shopping.
               * Shopping Agent Risk Assessment: The consensus is to avoid prioritizing the shopping agent with Temple and Webster currently, as the initial demo created excitement that cannot be immediately realized due to the complex internal approval process. There is a shared concern that the high barrier to entry could erode trust if the complex process does not lead to a successful outcome.
               * Agent Adoption and Organizational Management Plan: A joint session is proposed to address both the Gemini Enterprise roadmap and the organizational adoption of agents, focusing on how to manage and roll out agents effectively. This session will focus on giving select users access to low-code agents and creating a "mini champions group" to play with the platform and develop use cases. The goal is to establish "day zero value" by teaching champions from key business areas how to create and connect agents to files.
               * Session Scheduling and Key Takeaways for Temple and Webster: A session is currently booked for the 8th of April, suggested to allocate 30 minutes to the roadmap, 15 minutes to agent adoption, and 15 minutes for open discussion. It was agreed that the client should identify a champion from a core business area, and consulting with Remma is suggested to identify the right person for the call. The plan is to prioritize demonstrating successful, simple, low-risk use cases that automate processes on the "fringes" to build trust in the platform.
               * Tick Techch Enterprise Adoption Issues: Tick Techch is facing similar adoption issues with Gemini Enterprise, despite having bought 800 seats but only seeing about 20 users per week currently. A roadmap session has already been conducted with the CTO and Head of AI, meaning the next focus should be on the detailed steps for getting value from, building, and managing agents across different business units. A universal problem among customers is the substandard performance and reliability of Gemini Enterprise.

Temple and Webster Status and Strategy

               * A demo of the shopping agent using the client's merchant center data was performed, and the co-founder and CTO expressed keen interest.
               * Access Challenges: Access to the shopping agent is complicated by an internal process requiring a committed customer, a clear path to production rollout, and an aligned delivery team.

Adoption and Session Plan
               * Adoption Strategy: The participants proposed a joint session focusing on organizational adoption, including giving select users access to low-code agents and creating a "mini champions group" to develop use cases.
               * Goal: The aim is to establish "day zero value" by teaching champions from key business areas how to create and connect agents to files. The overall plan is to prioritize demonstrating simple, low-risk use cases that automate processes to build trust.
               * Scheduling: A session is booked for April 8th. It is suggested to allocate 30 minutes to the roadmap, 15 minutes to agent adoption, and 15 minutes for open discussion. Remma should be consulted to identify a champion from a core business area to attend.


Tick Techch Issues
               * Tick Techch is facing similar adoption issues with Gemini Enterprise, currently seeing only about 20 users per week despite buying 800 seats.
               * They are a GWS customer and delayed their Gemini Enterprise adoption until completing a GWS migration.
               * A universal problem cited among customers is the substandard performance and reliability of Gemini Enterprise.

Threshold Questions (from Adrian Giuffre)
Question 1: Data Source / Ecosystem
Which approved store will this be sourcing? (Select all that apply)
               * Google Workspace (Drive, Gmail, Calendar)
               * Atlassian Suite (Jira, Confluence)
               * Data Warehouse (BigQuery)
               * External/3rd Party API (e.g., Salesforce, ServiceNow, Slack) - Please specify:
Question 2: Data Classification (The Payload)
According to Nine’s Cyber Security Data Classification Policy, what is the HIGHEST level of data this agent will access, summarise, or process?
               * Public: (e.g., Published articles, press releases)
               * Internal: (e.g., General staff emails, operating procedures)
               * Confidential: (e.g., Unreleased M&A strategy, financial forecasts)
               * Restricted: (e.g., Personally Identifiable Information (PII), HR/Workday records, Tax File Numbers)
Question 3: AI Architecture (The Pipe)
How is Gemini interacting with this data?
               * Federated Search (Real-Time API): The AI only searches the source system in real-time when asked a question. Data is NOT copied into Google Cloud.
               * Data Ingestion (Storage): We are physically copying/ingesting this data from the source system into a Google Cloud Data Store (e.g., to build a knowledge graph).
Question 4: Data Store Permissions (Read vs. Write)
What permissions will this data store have access to within the source systems?
               * Read-Only: The agent can only search, summarise, and retrieve information.
               * Action/Write Capabilities: The agent will be granted API permissions to execute actions (e.g., Draft and send an email, update a Jira ticket, alter a database record, publish content).
Question 5: Audience & Use Case
Who is the end-user of this agent, and what is its primary function?
               * Internal Staff Only: Used strictly by Nine employees for internal productivity.
               * Public / Customer Facing: Used to interact directly with Nine consumers or the general public (e.g., a customer service chatbot).
               * Briefly describe the use case (1-2 sentences):

Meeting notes: Notes – Google + Stan/9Now Workshop


Notes:


OPEN QUESTIONS to Deliver our Roadmap 2026
                     * Media Search Performance & Latency
                     * What architectural optimizations (e.g., pre-computation, model pruning, or warming) are required to hit a <400ms SLI for the production environment, given the current latency is ~700ms?
                     * Model "Freshness" & Real-Time Adaptation
                     * What is the E2E data freshness latency window for Vertex AI Search & Recs, and how long before a trending Live Sport event or new TV episode influences Recommended For You (RFY)?
                     * Does Vertex AI support Online Learning/Streaming Ingestion for real-time session adaptation, or is Stan/9Now limited to daily batch-training cycles?
                     * Sport & Live Content Strategy
                     * Temporal Logic: How can Vertex AI be optimized for Live Sport to implement Time-to-Live (TTL) attributes that boost a match 2 hours before kickoff and automatically bury it after the final whistle?
                     * Parent/Child Asset Synchronization: How can Aggregated Asset Intelligence be implemented so that engagement on 'Child' assets (Highlights/Minis) contributes to the ranking of the 'Parent' series?
                     * Completion Rate Bias: How does the system handle Multi-Format Weighting within a single SeriesID to prevent the 'Average Completion Rate' from penalizing the parent asset's recommendation score if a user completes a 2-minute Highlight but skips a 2-hour Replay?
                     * Can architectural patterns from Global Sport SVODs be shared regarding how they handle 'Live-to-VOD' recommendations?
                     * Schema Expansion & Metadata
                     * Signal Diversification: How can custom intent signals like 'Add to My List,' 'Follow My Team,' or 'Trailer Completion' be ingested and weighted relative to 'Watch Time' within Serving Configurations, considering the current schema is limited to 5 standard event types?
                     * Request-Time Context: How can the 10-dimension limit in view events be leveraged to store Contextual Attributes (Device, Time of Day, Location) that can be passed at request-time to dynamically pivot the experience (e.g., 'Commuter/Mobile' vs. 'Home/Lean-back')?
                     * Does Vertex AI for Media now support a native User Profile Dataset, similar to Vertex AI for Retail, for storing long-term user preferences outside of the event stream?
                     * Environment & Data Management
                     * What are the best practices for Environment Isolation to 'clone' or 'branch' production Data Stores into a Sandbox/Staging environment for A/B simulations without impacting production billing, model stability, or existing datasets?
                     * Page-Level Orchestration & Ranker GA
                     * Are there plans to make the Ranker model General Availability, or to launch a 'Page-Level Optimisation' feature in Vertex AI?
                     * What are the Latency Service Level Indicators (SLIs) if the Ranker API is used to re-order 10+ Home page feeds at request-time in the interim?
                     * Share the Vertex AI roadmap 2026.


ACTIVITY - From Video Store to Merchandising
Merchandising (aka Full-Page Optimisation)
                     * Models: RFY, BYW for multiple feeds on a page.
                     * Different Stan/9Now objectives:
                     * Promotion (e.g., reach 500K views for Originals in Q4, reduce churn of customers at risk).
                     * User engagement (e.g., completion rate 70%).
                     * Vertex AI Model Business objective: CTR, Watch time per session, CVR.
                     * Config: Boost, bury, demote, diversity toggle.
                     * Orchestration:
                     * Dedup (front-end, complexity of codebases).
                     * Feed reordering.
                     * Operations: Enable Content Programming team to execute.


Parked topics
                     * Unified Ecosystem to Cross-Promote content (Stan + 9Now)
                     * The recommended architecture for Multi-Tenant Recommendations is being explored.
                     * The goal is to leverage user signals from 9Now to influence the Stan homepage (and vice-versa) without merging the entire catalogue.
                     * Current thinking favors using separate Stan and 9Now datasets and cross-promoting with both platforms' titles to avoid affecting performance of platform-specific recommendations.

Discussion confirmed the session ID fix rollout for Gemini Enterprise, with clarification on default ADK session and memory artifacts.


Session ID Bug Resolution
The issue regarding the Gemini Enterprise session ID changing with every message has been identified, and the fix is currently being deployed. The core issue of inconsistent session management appears to be solved, with observed consistency in session IDs and tracing.


Default ADK Session Clarity
Clarification confirmed that when environment variables are set for Vertex, the default ADK app object automatically instantiates the correct session service for memory and sessions. This negates the need for explicitly declaring memory and session artifacts builders during deployment.


Statefulness and Customer Communication
It was agreed that new stateful projects should no longer require immediate allow listing for the session ID fix. The decision was made that Joshua Gibbs should communicate the resolved statefulness issue to the Greenstone client.

Details
                        * Session ID Bug Resolution and Implementation Details: The discussion confirmed that the issue regarding the Gemini Enterprise session ID changing with every message to the deployed agent engine has been identified and is being rolled out as a fix. Joshua Gibbs observed that the fix appears to be working in their testing, although they were unsure if a large change was made to Gemini Enterprise or if their project was merely allow listed for the new implementation. Pedro Correia confirmed that when environment variables are set up for Vertex, it automatically instantiates the correct session service, meaning that the default ADK app object should use the correct memory and sessions when deploying via Agent Engine's create argument.

Pedro Correia stated that they suspect the fix will be included in broader changes to observability on Gemini Enterprise scheduled for Q2, but that they did not observe the issue in their current project, suggesting it might already be solved.

Pedro Correia and Phoebe Gunter agreed that the bug should no longer be present, but if it reappears, Joshua Gibbs should message Phoebe Gunter, and the issue could be addressed quickly. Joshua Gibbs noted that they would now need to make changes to utilize the newly restored stateful solution, moving away from their previous workaround of using verbose output of bucket locations for artifacts to maintain state.

The following are expectations for operational excellence and the forecast call structure:


Consumption Culture and PBO Model
                        * Sellers will own the end-to-end sales and consumption cycle.
                        * The focus is on accelerating the conversion of bookings backlog into revenue-generating workloads.

Bi-Weekly Team Meeting & Forecast Call Agenda
The bi-weekly meeting is structured as follows:
                        * 5 mins: Good news stories
                        * 10 mins: Important Cluster Updates (Partner, Marketing, Events etc)
                        * 5 mins: Cluster PBO overview (All Rev and DA/DB/Cloud AI), Sec&Wk, GE and Commit Bookings
                        * 60 mins: Top 20 Workloads (Stage 3 and In-Q)
                        * 20 mins: Review Top 5 GE deals, Top 5 Sec&Workspace Bookings, and Top 5 Marketplace opportunities
                        * 20 mins: Other Key Workload callouts
Pipeline Management Expectations for Forecast Call Preparation
                        * All workloads must be captured and up to date with PILLAR, SOLUTION, SALES PLAY, STAGE, VALUE, START DATE, RAMP DATE, SALES & CE WORKLOAD OWNER recorded.

nine - ai + da
Description: Archive modernization project focuses on AI consumption and data opportunities, with BigQuery integration and cost-effectiveness challenges discussed.


Attachments:
                        * Meeting Notes/Transcript
                        * Nine Digital Library Architecture
Ateendees: thompsoni@google.com johnwhyte@google.com karimawan@google.com russnash@google.com pcorreia@google.com
Meeting notes:


Notes:


Summary
The archive modernization project focuses on AI consumption and data opportunities, with BigQuery integration and cost-effectiveness challenges discussed.


AI Data Archive Potential
The project addresses both AI and data focus areas across the sales play, aiming to secure AI consumption even without winning the storage component. Opportunities include storage, compute, and industry solutions like search and recommendation.


Architecture and Use Cases
The current proprietary publishing tool limits the archive depth to five years. The proposal is to replace the existing search system with Gemini for richer functionalities. The primary internal use case is semantic search and a knowledge base, which requires better metadata and engagement with product managers.


Cost and Commercial Requirements
A critical challenge is reducing the current digitization cost from 68 cents per page to less than 10 cents for metadata extraction and article reconstruction. Project entry is contingent upon winning the storage component. The projected cost for storage and BigQuery slots is around $500,000, while the Gemini income is only projected at about $60,000.


Next steps
                        * [The group] Engage PMs: Engage Nine product managers to discuss product catalog strategy.
                        * [Someone in Heron (au-syd-pir)] Analyze Cost: Perform cost analysis on 10 provided pages to calculate costs to be less than 10 cents per page.


Details
                        * The project is focused on having an archive, applying to broadcasters and newspapers like Nine and ABC.
                        * Current architecture only reflects GCS storage and AI consumption, lacking BigQuery, which Nine requires. BigQuery is considered the best option for metadata, although a document database is possible.
                        * The first AI use case is internal semantic search and a knowledge base; phase two will add archival search features to apps like the digital Sydney Morning Herald.
                        * Key stakeholders include Kelly Dudley (AI and data). The library team reports to Ian Harris (head of platforms), whose compensation is linked to consumption. A key team member, Justin, has a strong preference for Databricks.
                        * The existing article search tool, INC, uses Vertex AI Search connected to a BigQuery dataset holding metadata for articles written since INC was created, limiting the archive to about five years.
                        * The proposed solution involves replacing Vertex AI Search with Gemini to enable richer functionalities like agents and timeline creators beyond simple search.
                        * The client needs a solution to replace Continuum’s work, which charges 68 cents per page, with a cost of less than 10 cents per page for metadata extraction and article reconstruction.
                        * The archive includes daily newspapers (four versions), images within PDFs (e.g., film strips), and video content (news bulletins, reality shows). The team is allow-listed for sensitive data processing to create metadata.
                        * Video content processing should use "chapterization" based on context (key moments) for organization. While an event-driven architecture using Firestore was demonstrated for video metadata generation, rebuilding the system using BigQuery was recommended due to improved vector search capability.
                        * The primary internal benefit is quicker content creation for journalists, allowing them to monetize articles faster. The long-term plan is a business-to-consumer (B2C) model allowing independent journalists to pay a monthly fee for archive access.
                        * The proposed processing pipeline is modality-agnostic. It uses BigQuery with an adaptive JSON schema to hold metadata for different modalities (images, articles, video) in one table, enabling cross-modality search. New BigQuery capabilities, including multimodal embedding support from Gemini, eliminate the need for a separate vector store.
                        * Multimodal embedding for images is feasible, but for video, it is cost-prohibitive unless the video is chunked (e.g., 120 seconds).
                        * The document outlining the solution, including a proposed BigQuery schema and data flow graph, has not yet been presented to the client.


Gemini Feedback on Nine Digital Library Architecture
                        1. The "Quality Control" (HITL) Layer: A Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) interface is missing. The recommendation is to add a feedback loop for journalists to flag or correct AI-generated metadata, which should feed back into BigQuery to refine the Knowledge Graph.
                        2. Observability & Dead Letter Queues: Error handling and monitoring components are missing. It is recommended to implement Pub/Sub Dead Letter Topics for failed Cloud Run Jobs and use a Cloud Monitoring Dashboard to track real-time Gemini quota usage.
                        3. Data Ingress (The "Physical to Cloud" Bridge): The strategy for ingress of the 8,500 TiB physical archive is missing. It is recommended to detail the process using tools like Google Cloud Transfer Appliances for the bulk backlog.
                        4. Security & Data Governance: IAM and Encryption details are missing. It is recommended to define Workload Identity for Cloud Run Jobs (Read GCS, Write BigQuery) and consider Cloud KMS if required for controlling encryption of the 8.5 PB backlog.
                        5. Multi-Region vs. Regional Resilience: Disaster Recovery (DR) plans are missing. The recommendation is to specify if the archive will be stored in a Multi-Region bucket or if a secondary region is used for backup of the most critical metadata.
                        6. The "Discovery" Layer (Front-End): A front-end discovery layer is missing. It is recommended to point Vertex AI Search directly at the BigQuery metadata table to provide an "Internal Google" for the archive, including citations and natural language answers.

The initial top-down purchase decision was questioned by staff below Kelly Dudley. The company has since refocused and is now keen to proceed with assistance for product differentiation and rollout planning.


Finalizing Rollout and Use Cases
The Gemini Enterprise rollout plan includes a champions group and structured change management. The goal is to distribute all 2,250 seats by June alongside 2 ADK agents. Strategic focus is shifting to enabling broader use cases, such as connecting data sources like Jira and BigQuery, to drive adoption beyond the current sold seats and reach a potential of 4,000 seats.

Nerissa Penfold suggested that Bashwan, a SASOC, might own the ramp-up from a customer perspective, rather than Kim Kareem. The role of the SASOCs is to take primary ownership for some ramp activities when an OCE is present.

Nerissa Penfold emphasized the need to clarify roles, as the expectation was that the OCE would take on that leadership role for future activities.

The dynamic has shifted because Kelly Dudley is now "vested in the platform". The team planning the rollout for the next day includes Ebony, Danica (who is leaving), and Soha (who is replacing Danica).
                        * Guidance for Tomorrow's Meeting and Clarifying Roles: Nerissa Penfold asked for guidance on the meeting, noting that Ebony is "notoriously cold" and is better in person. Pedro Correia recommended reviewing the customer's plan and trying to build a bridge between the Google approach and their plan. It was also deemed crucial to clarify the roles of the substantial internal team involved.

ADK / Agent Building with Justin
Description:


Attachments: - Meeting Notes Document


Ateendees: david.ngo@templeandwebster.com.au rjerrems@google.com jbolilia@google.com amyrom@google.com rima.chaudhuri@templeandwebster.com.au
Meeting notes:


Notes:


Summary
Meeting focused on fact-finding for AI agent development covering architecture and environment synchronization issues.


Strategic AI Agent Investment
Temple Webster's AI agent investment covers internal productivity, personal augmentation, and agent-as-a-product categories, with the primary focus currently on automating manual pipelines for internal productivity gains. The Insights Analyst agent aims to empower employees to perform complex data queries without manual SQL knowledge against a PII-scrubbed dataset.


Agent Engine Deployment Challenges
A critical challenge involves ADK rapid prototypes failing upon deployment to Agent Engine or Gemini Enterprise, requiring better environment synchronization between the development and runtime environments. The proposed solution is a "bring your own container" approach using a custom Docker file to match the local ADK environment to the agent runtime.


Architecture and Tooling Guidance
It was recommended that generated artifacts be stored in external storage, such as GCS, for better auditability and context management, instead of being stored in memory. Advice for Managed Compute Platform (MCP) server guidance suggested employing multiple MCPs across specific domains to prevent overwhelming the agent context.


Next steps
                           * [Justin Bolilia] Share Container Docs: Send documentation regarding the bring your own container solution. Work with Rohan Jerrems to synchronize development environments.
                           * [Justin Bolilia] Research Artifact Retrieval: Investigate how other customer artifact loading issues progressed. Confirm if Gemini Enterprise picks up external storage (GCS).
                           * [Justin Bolilia] Investigate LLM Agent: Review the specific artifact rendering issue when switching from LLM Agent to Agent classes. Cross reference findings with artifact loading problems.
                           * [Justin Bolilia] Follow Up: Find another time with David to discuss rendering and specific agent optimizations.
                           * [Rohan Jerrems] Schedule Meetings: Line up follow-up discussions for Rima/Justin and David/Justin. Talk offline with Justin to determine the best time.

Meeting notes:


Notes:
The following key notes were extracted from the AuNZ CE Team Huddle presentation deck:
                           * AlphaFold 3: This is a major evolution from AlphaFold 2, modeling all biological molecule interactions (proteins, DNA, RNA, ligands, ions, etc.). It uses an improved Evoformer module and a diffusion network, and its predictions surpass existing specialized systems.
                           * BigQuery Cost Management: BigQuery Markup Language (BQML) workloads are almost always better run on Editions (slots-based) pricing rather than on-demand to prevent accidental overspend. One customer accidentally spent $2.4M across two days due to running BQML jobs on on-demand pricing.
                           * Cultural Content: It is traditional in New Zealand to begin meetings with a Karakia (a secular incantation aimed at grounding and focusing), complementing the Australian tradition of acknowledging the country.
                           * Dev Competition: The Simplify & Accelerate: The GCP DX Challenge competition runs from July 21st to August 15th, with categories including "Flow Master," "AI Innovator," and "Problem Solver". Prizes are planned for winners.
                           * GenSec AI: This capability infuses AI into security workflows using Gemini and SecLM, a specialized Large Language Model trained on threat intelligence.
                           * Google Unified Security: This platform converges Google's core security offerings—threat visibility, SecOps, cloud security, and Mandiant expertise—powered by Gemini AI and running on the Security Data Fabric. Mandiant consulting offers Incident Response, Strategic Readiness, Offensive Security, and Cybersecurity Transformation services.
                           * Outcome CE (OCE) Team: The CE organization is shifting from a consultative model to a focused execution structure. The OCE team's mandate is to accelerate customer value realization and revenue growth by ensuring the rapid ramp of committed workloads into production.
                           * Web Risk Policy: For API submission, the minimum deal size is $100K. Deals between $100K and $250K only receive CE support for Proof of Concept (POC), while deals over $250K receive CE support plus engineering support.
                           * Workload Identity Federation (WLIF): Since early 2024, an enhancement was introduced allowing users to optionally skip the step of impersonating a GCP Service Account when configuring WLIF.
